ZhoupersName, X. J. ZhupersName, Aaron ZimmermanpersName, Yosef ZlochowerpersName, M. E. ZuckerpersName, J. ZweizigpersName (2019date). “GWTC-1: A Gravitational-Wave Transient Catalog of Compact Binary Mergers Observed by LIGO and Virgo during the First and Second Observing Runs.” Physical Review Xtitle·venue.

Object of study — every candidate compact-binary merger signal in the LIGO-Virgo data from the first two observing runs - the 11 the collaboration is confident about and the 14 it is not

Research question — which candidates in O1 and O2 are of astrophysical rather than terrestrial origin, at what significance, and what merger rates follow from the answer

Contribution — the first LIGO-Virgo transient catalog: four previously unpublished binary black hole mergers, updated significances and parameters for every earlier detection, and an explicit list of the marginal candidates that were not resolved

Cross-disciplinary analysis
content-tags & facets: phenomenon · claim-type · cross-domain-transfer · mechanism · claim-relation · replication-status · limitation — grounded in the full text
phenomenongravitational waves from the inspiral, merger and ringdown of compact binary coalescences, detected by a network of ground-based interferometers — “This network of ground-based interferometric detectors is sensitive to GWs from the inspiral, merger, and ringdown of compact binary coalescences (CBCs), covering a frequency range from about 15 Hz up to a few kilohertz”
claim-typeexistence — “In this catalog, we report 11 confident detections of GWs from compact binary mergers as well as a selection of less significant triggers from both observing runs.”
method — evidence — “The events presented here are obtained from a total of three searches: two matched-filter searches, PyCBC [7,8] and GstLAL [9,10], using relativistic models of GWs from CBCs, as well as one unmodeled search for short-duration transient signals or bursts, coherent WaveBurst (cWB) [11].”
object of study — evidence — “In this catalog, we report 11 confident detections of GWs from compact binary mergers as well as a selection of less significant triggers from both observing runs.”
research question — evidence — “To do this identification, we establish an initial threshold on estimated FAR of 1 per 30 days (about 12.2 per year), excluding any event that does not have a FAR less than this threshold in at least one of the two matched-filter analyses (see Sec. III).”
contribution — evidence — “In this catalog, we announce four previously unpublished BBH mergers observed during O2: GW170729, GW170809, GW170818, and GW170823.”
mechanismscattered light light scattered inside the interferometer puts excess power at low frequencies, appearing as arch shapes in a spectrogram; where those frequencies reach the analysis band it can produce a significant matched-filter trigger — “Scattered light leads to excess power at low frequencies that appear in time-frequency spectrograms as archlike shapes. In some cases, the frequencies affected are above the minimum frequency used in the analysis. When this happens, scattered light transients can create significant triggers in matched-filter searches [68,92,93].”
mechanismground motion storms and earthquakes drive high-amplitude ground motion which both destabilises the interferometer and generates the scattering artifacts, so the environmental cause and the data artifact arrive together — “The presence of intense scattering artifacts contribute to the unstable state of the interferometer and could account for the SNR of the marginal trigger.”
mechanismtemplate density why noise triggers pile up at low chirp mass - the template bank is much more densely packed there, so noise has more chances to match something — “The concentration of our marginal triggers at low chirp masses is consistent with expectations for noise triggers, because search template waveforms are much more densely packed at low masses.”
mechanismarrival-time difference sky position is reconstructed from the difference in arrival time across separated detector sites, which is why adding Virgo to the two LIGO sites improves localisation so sharply — “As the sky location is primarily determined by the differences in the times of arrival of the GW signal at the different detector sites, LIGO-Virgo coincident events have a vastly improved sky localization, which is crucial for electromagnetic follow-up campaigns”
mechanismpipeline-specific noise a noise trigger is a function of the pipeline that found it, so the same noise is not expected to appear in both searches - which is why agreement between pipelines carries information and disagreement does not immediately count against a candidate — “As noise triggers are generically a function of the details of the pipeline that identifies a trigger, we do not typically expect to see the same noise triggers in each pipeline.”
extends the individual LIGO-Virgo detection papers for GW150914, GW151226, GW170104, GW170608, GW170814 and GW170817, whose significances and parameter estimates this catalog replaces — “The updated results we report here supersede those previously published.”
confirms an independent catalog by another group which reanalysed the O1 data with the PyCBC pipeline and found the same three events, with identical FAR bounds for two of them — “That catalog also presents search results from the PyCBC pipeline for O1 and also finds GW150914, GW151012, and GW151226 as the only confident gravitational-wave events in O1, with identical bounds on FAR to the PyCBC results in Table I for GW150914 and GW151226.”
qualifies the collaboration's own earlier designation of LVT151012 as a trigger rather than an event, which the improved pipelines overturn — “this event meets the criteria of Sec. IVA for a gravitational-wave event, and we henceforth relabel this event as GW151012.”
senseweakly modeled - THE PAPER COINS THIS ABOUT ITSELF AND SAYS WHY. cWB is introduced as an unmodeled search, but the analysis reported here restricts it to signals that chirp in the time-frequency plane, so it is no longer model-free. The authors change the word rather than keep the stronger one — “In contrast, the cWB search relies on no specific physical models of the source waveform, though in results presented here it does impose a restriction that signals are “chirping” in the time-frequency plane. We therefore refer to it as weakly modeled.”
sensemarginal - NEITHER ESTABLISHED NOR EXCLUDED, and the paper holds that line to the end. A marginal trigger passed the false-alarm threshold but was given less than a 50% probability of astrophysical origin by both matched-filter searches. It is not a non-detection and not a weak detection: it is a candidate the collaboration declines to resolve, and it says so in the conclusions as well as the results — “In addition, we list 14 GW candidate events that have an estimated false-alarm rate (FAR) less than 1 per 30 days in either of the two matched-filter analyses but whose astrophysical origin cannot be established nor excluded unambiguously (Sec. VII).”
sensefalse-alarm rate - AN EMPIRICAL RATE MEASURED AGAINST TIME-SHIFTED DATA, NOT A PROBABILITY THAT THE EVENT IS NOISE. Where a candidate outranks every background trigger the search can only report a BOUND, and the bound depends on how much background time was available - which is why some FARs here are larger than the ones previously published for the same events — “This restriction means, however, the minimum bound on the false-alarm rate of candidates that have a higher ranking statistic than any trigger in the background sample is larger than it would be if longer periods of data are used for the time-shift analysis.”
senseindependent, of the three searches - INDEPENDENCE OF METHOD, NOT OF EVIDENCE. The conclusions say `three independent analyses` flatly; what is independent is the pipeline, the ranking statistic and the noise model, while the strain data are the same in all three. The body is more careful than the conclusion, which is worth knowing before the agreement between them is read as three confirmations — “The signals were discovered using three independent analyses: two matched-filter searches [8,9] and one weakly modeled burst search [11].”
senseGW versus marginal - A THRESHOLD CONVENTION, NOT A PROPERTY OF THE UNIVERSE. The boundary is a false-alarm rate ceiling of 1 per 30 days followed by a 50% astrophysical probability, both chosen by the collaboration. An event just under the line and one just over it differ by the convention, and the paper presents the choice as a choice — “we assign the “GW” designation to any event for which the probability of astrophysical origin from either matched-filter search is greater than 50%”
sensedetection, in the phrase `no new gravitational-wave events were discovered` - THE O1 REANALYSIS FOUND NOTHING NEW BUT DID CHANGE AN OLD ANSWER. The same data through better pipelines moved LVT151012 across the threshold and it is now GW151012. So the null result is about new events only; the catalog's contents still changed — “These improved FAR measurements for GW151012 are the most salient result of the reanalysis of O1 with the O2 pipelines; no new gravitational-wave events were discovered.”
controlsignificance measured against a background built by repeatedly time-shifting one detector's triggers against the other's and counting the coincidences that result — held — “For an estimation of its statistical significance, each candidate event is ranked against a sample of background triggers obtained by repeating the analysis on time-shifted data, similar to the background estimation in the PyCBC search.” — rules out a chance alignment of independent noise in two detectors being read as a signal - the candidate is ranked against a background of coincidences manufactured from the same data — “For an estimation of its statistical significance, each candidate event is ranked against a sample of background triggers obtained by repeating the analysis on time-shifted data, similar to the background estimation in the PyCBC search.”
controlthe time shifts are chosen much larger than the light-travel delay between sites — held — “To exclude astrophysical events from the background sample, the time shifts are selected to be much larger than the expected signal delay between the detectors.” — rules out a real astrophysical signal leaking into the background sample and inflating the false-alarm rate it is being measured against — “To exclude astrophysical events from the background sample, the time shifts are selected to be much larger than the expected signal delay between the detectors.”
controlGstLAL's noise model built from single-detector triggers that were NOT found in coincidence — held — “is constructed, in part, from single-detector triggers that are not found in coincidence, so as to minimize the possibility of contamination by real signals.” — rules out real signals contaminating the model of what noise looks like - the same concern as the time shifts, met by a different construction — “is constructed, in part, from single-detector triggers that are not found in coincidence, so as to minimize the possibility of contamination by real signals.”
controla veto on the network correlation coefficient, rejecting candidates below 0.7 — held — “Typically, for a GW signal cc ≈ 1, and for instrumental glitches cc ≪ 1. Therefore, candidate events with cc<0.7 are rejected as potential glitches.” — rules out instrumental glitches - the statistic sits near 1 for a real signal and far below it for a glitch, so a floor at 0.7 removes the second without a model of either — “Typically, for a GW signal cc ≈ 1, and for instrumental glitches cc ≪ 1. Therefore, candidate events with cc<0.7 are rejected as potential glitches.”
controla minimum waveform duration of 0.15 s, which sets the high-mass edge of the search — held — “The high-mass boundary of the search space is determined by the requirement that the waveform duration be at least 0.15 s, which reduces the number of false-alarm triggers from short instrumental glitches.” — rules out short instrumental glitches ranking highly against the very short templates a higher mass limit would require — “The high-mass boundary of the search space is determined by the requirement that the waveform duration be at least 0.15 s, which reduces the number of false-alarm triggers from short instrumental glitches.”
controltwo signal-based vetoes applied to PyCBC single-detector triggers — held — “In order to suppress triggers caused by high-amplitude noise transients (“glitches”), two signal-based vetoes may be calculated [71,72].” — rules out high-amplitude noise transients producing triggers that look like signals to the matched filter — “In order to suppress triggers caused by high-amplitude noise transients (“glitches”), two signal-based vetoes may be calculated [71,72].”
controltime shifts restricted to within a single analysis period rather than across the whole run — partial — “This restriction means, however, the minimum bound on the false-alarm rate of candidates that have a higher ranking statistic than any trigger in the background sample is larger than it would be if longer periods of data are used for the time-shift analysis.” — rules out a background averaged over detector states that differ - the noise changes substantially between the start of O1 and the end of O2. IT DOES NOT COME FREE and the authors say so: the reachable lower bound on the false-alarm rate gets larger — “the results here consider only time shifts within a given analysis period, which is done because the noise characteristics of the detector vary significantly from the beginning of O1 through the end of O2, so this restriction more accurately reflects the variation in detector performance.”
controla detector-characterisation investigation of every marginal trigger, using auxiliary sensors alongside the strain data, against two stated metrics — partial — “Of the marginal triggers presented in this catalog, nine have excess power from known sources of noise occurring during times when the matched-filter template that yields the trigger has a GW frequency within the sensitive band of the detectors. For four of these cases, the observed instrumental artifact overlaps the signal region and possibly causes the marginal trigger.” — rules out a known instrumental or environmental artifact being the whole explanation of a marginal trigger. PARTIAL BY THE PAPER'S OWN COUNT: nine of the fourteen coincide with known noise and four of those could account for the trigger — “Using auxiliary sensors at each detector, as well as the gravitational-wave strain data, we evaluate the state of the detectors at the time of each marginal trigger, identify and investigate any artifacts in the data due to noise, and test whether any identified artifacts might explain the excess SNR observed in the analysis.”
controlglitch subtraction followed by reanalysis of the cleaned data - the counterfactual rather than the correlation — held — “In both cases, the marginal trigger remains with similar significance, suggesting that the observed scattering artifact could not have accounted for the SNR of the marginal trigger.” — rules out scattered light as the cause of the two triggers examined this way - with the artifact subtracted the trigger survives at similar significance, so the artifact was not producing it — “In both cases, the marginal trigger remains with similar significance, suggesting that the observed scattering artifact could not have accounted for the SNR of the marginal trigger.”
controla Poisson calculation on the clustering of five events into August 2017 — held — “The probability that a Poisson process would produce five events or more in at least one of those periods is 5.3%. Thus, seeing five events in one month is statistically consistent with expectations.” — rules out a run of detections in one month being read as an excess over the expected rate - the probability of it happening in at least one of the ten comparable periods is 5.3% — “The probability that a Poisson process would produce five events or more in at least one of those periods is 5.3%. Thus, seeing five events in one month is statistically consistent with expectations.”
controlan independent waveform reconstruction using wavelets and the unmodelled search, compared against the template-based reconstruction, both visually and by subtracting the best-fit waveform and reanalysing — held — “Both comparisons agree within expectations from analysis of simulations, and we conclude that there are no detectable discrepancies between the wavelet- and template-based reconstructions” — rules out the inferred signal being an artefact of the relativistic waveform models - which the authors introduce precisely by saying those models may not include all physics — “Quantitative comparisons of the template- and wavelet-based methods include computing overlaps between the reconstructions and reanalyzing the data with the wavelet-based analysis after the best-fit CBC waveform has been subtracted.”
controlchecking that features appearing in the wavelet reconstruction's 90% band are absent from its 50% band, and reproducing them with simulated signals injected into real data — held — “Analogous features are seen when reconstructing simulated signals added to real data and should not be misinterpreted as evidence for disagreement with the template-based reconstructions.” — rules out apparent extra structure in the reconstruction being read as disagreement with the template model - it is coherent noise, and it appears the same way for signals that are known to be real because they were injected — “We verify that these outliers are absent from the BAYESWAVE 50% credible intervals (not shown here), indicating that they have low significance.”
controlthe pipeline's response calibrated by recovering injected synthetic signals — held — “The astrophysical probabilities from GstLAL in Table IV are estimated using the pipeline response to injected synthetic signals, where neutron stars are assumed to have masses in the range 1–3 M⊙ and black holes are assumed to have masses of 3 M⊙ or larger.” — rules out the astrophysical probabilities resting on an assumed rather than a measured detection efficiency — “The astrophysical probabilities from GstLAL in Table IV are estimated using the pipeline response to injected synthetic signals, where neutron stars are assumed to have masses in the range 1–3 M⊙ and black holes are assumed to have masses of 3 M⊙ or larger.”
controlthe unmodelled search's results excluded from the event-selection step — held — “The cWB search results are not used in the event selection process.” — rules out the event list depending on three different ranking statistics with three different noise models - selection rests on the two matched-filter searches only — “The cWB search results are not used in the event selection process.”
notePyCBC —distinct-from→ GstLAL — both are matched-filter searches on the same data; what differs is the ranking statistic, the noise model and the template bank. The paper's word is `largely independent`, and the weight is below 1.0 for that reason — “The events presented here are obtained from a total of three searches: two matched-filter searches, PyCBC [7,8] and GstLAL [9,10], using relativistic models of GWs from CBCs, as well as one unmodeled search for short-duration transient signals or bursts, coherent WaveBurst (cWB) [11].”
weightPyCBC —distinct-from→ GstLAL — 0.9 — “The events presented here are obtained from a total of three searches: two matched-filter searches, PyCBC [7,8] and GstLAL [9,10], using relativistic models of GWs from CBCs, as well as one unmodeled search for short-duration transient signals or bursts, coherent WaveBurst (cWB) [11].”
weightmatched-filter searches —contrasted-with→ unmodeled search — 0.95 — “The events presented here are obtained from a total of three searches: two matched-filter searches, PyCBC [7,8] and GstLAL [9,10], using relativistic models of GWs from CBCs, as well as one unmodeled search for short-duration transient signals or bursts, coherent WaveBurst (cWB) [11].”
notecWB —is-a→ unmodeled search — and the paper immediately withdraws the word for itself - see the `weakly modeled` sense. The edge records what cWB is called at first mention, not what it is — “The events presented here are obtained from a total of three searches: two matched-filter searches, PyCBC [7,8] and GstLAL [9,10], using relativistic models of GWs from CBCs, as well as one unmodeled search for short-duration transient signals or bursts, coherent WaveBurst (cWB) [11].”
weightcWB —is-a→ unmodeled search — 0.85 — “The events presented here are obtained from a total of three searches: two matched-filter searches, PyCBC [7,8] and GstLAL [9,10], using relativistic models of GWs from CBCs, as well as one unmodeled search for short-duration transient signals or bursts, coherent WaveBurst (cWB) [11].”
notefalse-alarm rate —operationalised-as→ background triggers — the FAR is the rate of background triggers ranking at least as high as the candidate; the background is manufactured by time shifting, so the measure exists only relative to how much shifted time was analysed — “Each foreground coincident assigned a false-alarm rate (FAR) given by the number of background triggers with an equal or larger ranking, divided by the total time searched for time-shifted coincidences.”
weightfalse-alarm rate —operationalised-as→ background triggers — 0.95 — “Each foreground coincident assigned a false-alarm rate (FAR) given by the number of background triggers with an equal or larger ranking, divided by the total time searched for time-shifted coincidences.”
edgebackground triggers —operationalised-as→ time-shifted data — “For an estimation of its statistical significance, each candidate event is ranked against a sample of background triggers obtained by repeating the analysis on time-shifted data, similar to the background estimation in the PyCBC search.”
effect directionscattered light —causes→ triggers — increases — “Scattered light leads to excess power at low frequencies that appear in time-frequency spectrograms as archlike shapes. In some cases, the frequencies affected are above the minimum frequency used in the analysis. When this happens, scattered light transients can create significant triggers in matched-filter searches [68,92,93].” — “scattered light transients can create significant triggers in matched-filter searches”
weightscattered light —causes→ triggers — 0.9 — “Scattered light leads to excess power at low frequencies that appear in time-frequency spectrograms as archlike shapes. In some cases, the frequencies affected are above the minimum frequency used in the analysis. When this happens, scattered light transients can create significant triggers in matched-filter searches [68,92,93].”
effect directionglitches —causes→ triggers — increases — “The high-mass boundary of the search space is determined by the requirement that the waveform duration be at least 0.15 s, which reduces the number of false-alarm triggers from short instrumental glitches.” — “reduces the number of false-alarm triggers from short instrumental glitches”
noteglitches —causes→ triggers — signed from the direction of the REMEDY: requiring a longer waveform reduces the glitch-driven false alarms, so the glitches were raising them — “The high-mass boundary of the search space is determined by the requirement that the waveform duration be at least 0.15 s, which reduces the number of false-alarm triggers from short instrumental glitches.”
weightglitches —causes→ triggers — 0.8 — “The high-mass boundary of the search space is determined by the requirement that the waveform duration be at least 0.15 s, which reduces the number of false-alarm triggers from short instrumental glitches.”
effect directionsignal-based vetoes —causes→ triggers — reduces — “In order to suppress triggers caused by high-amplitude noise transients (“glitches”), two signal-based vetoes may be calculated [71,72].” — “In order to suppress triggers caused by high-amplitude noise transients”
notesignal-based vetoes —causes→ triggers — the vetoes act on the TRIGGERS, not on the glitches - the noise transients are still in the instrument afterwards. Pointed here in the audit, where the edge had read `vetoes reduce glitches`, which the paper does not say and which is not true — “In order to suppress triggers caused by high-amplitude noise transients (“glitches”), two signal-based vetoes may be calculated [71,72].”
weightsignal-based vetoes —causes→ triggers — 0.9 — “In order to suppress triggers caused by high-amplitude noise transients (“glitches”), two signal-based vetoes may be calculated [71,72].”

limitationthe 14 marginal triggers cannot be resolved individually - some may be real and the analysis cannot say which — “Although it may be the case that some of these marginal triggers are of astrophysical origin, we cannot then determine which ones.”
limitationrestated in the conclusions rather than left in the results: the COUNT of marginal events is as expected, and no particular one can be called signal or noise — “The number of observed marginal events is consistent with our expectation given the chosen FAR threshold, but it is not possible to say whether or not a particular marginal trigger is a real GW signal.”
limitationwhere a candidate outranks the whole time-shifted background, the false-alarm rate is only a bound, and shortening the time-shift periods made that bound larger than the one previously published for the same events — “This restriction means, however, the minimum bound on the false-alarm rate of candidates that have a higher ranking statistic than any trigger in the background sample is larger than it would be if longer periods of data are used for the time-shift analysis.”
limitationthe source properties rest on relativistic waveform models which the authors say do not necessarily incorporate all physical effects — “Such models, however, do not necessarily incorporate all physical effects.”
limitationthe matched-filter searches assume quasicircular binaries with spins aligned or antialigned to the orbital angular momentum - a restriction on the template bank, though the authors note precessing systems are still often recovered — “The two matched-filter searches assume sources that can be modeled by general relativity and, in particular, quasicircular binaries whose spin angular momenta are either aligned or antialigned with their orbital angular momenta. They are still capable, however, of detecting many systems that exhibit precession [69].”
limitationindividual black hole spins are only weakly constrained — “Similar to previous results, we find that the spins of the individual black holes are only weakly constrained”
Content tags
content-observations: finding · quantity · variable · definition · key-term — grounded in the full text
finding11 confident detections across O1 and O2: ten binary black hole mergers and one binary neutron star inspiral — “In this catalog, we report 11 confident detections of GWs from compact binary mergers as well as a selection of less significant triggers from both observing runs.”
findingfour binary black hole mergers announced here for the first time: GW170729, GW170809, GW170818, GW170823 — “In this catalog, we announce four previously unpublished BBH mergers observed during O2: GW170729, GW170809, GW170818, and GW170823.”
findingNULL RESULT: no neutron star-black hole merger was detected, which is what the rate upper limit is built from — “No neutron star–black hole mergers were detected.”
findingNULL RESULT: reanalysing O1 with the improved pipelines discovered no new events at all - but it did raise GW151012's significance enough to relabel it — “These improved FAR measurements for GW151012 are the most salient result of the reanalysis of O1 with the O2 pipelines; no new gravitational-wave events were discovered.”
findingNULL RESULT: no binary component has been observed in either putative mass gap - the one between neutron stars and black holes, or the pair-instability gap — “To date, no binary components have been observed in either of the two putative mass gaps [139,140]—one between NSs and BHs and the other one due to pair instability supernovae [136,219].”
finding14 candidates passed the false-alarm threshold but were given under 50% astrophysical probability by both matched-filter searches, and are published unresolved — “In addition, we list 14 GW candidate events that have an estimated false-alarm rate (FAR) less than 1 per 30 days in either of the two matched-filter analyses but whose astrophysical origin cannot be established nor excluded unambiguously (Sec. VII).”

III A, this search elects to use shorter periods of time shifting to better capture the variation in the detectors”

quantity — — “Though the probability that 11 triggers could arise only from noise when six are expected is low, it is by itself not sufficiently low to confidently assert that some fraction of these triggers are astrophysical in origin.”

Statistics — cross-field join keys
field-invariant statistical terms, used in their statistical sense (gated on a number / symbol / test). A p-value or a standard deviation means the SAME thing in physics, biology, psychology and sociology — so these are the strongest cross-field join keys. Grounded verbatim.
— “The probability that a Poisson process would produce five events or more in at least one of those periods is 5.3%. Thus, seeing five events in one month is statistically consistent with expectations.”
— “At this FAR threshold, if each pipeline produces independent noise events, we would expect on average two such noise events (false alarms) per month of analyzed coincident time. During these first two observing runs, we also empirically observe approximately two likely signal events per month of analyzed time. Thus, for O1 and O2, any sample of events all of whose measured FARs are greater than 1 per 30 days is expected to consist of at least 50% noise triggers.”

PHYSICAL REVIEW X 9, 031040 (2019)

GWTC-1: A Gravitational-Wave Transient Catalog of Compact Binary Mergers Observed by LIGO and Virgo during the First and Second Observing Runs

B. P. AbbottpersName et al.* (LIGO Scientific Collaboration and Virgo Collaboration)

(Received 14 December 2018; revised manuscript received 27 March 2019; published 4 September 2019)

We present the results from three gravitational-wave searches for coalescing compact binaries with component masses above 1 M⊙ during the first and second observing runs of the advanced gravitationalwave detector network. During the first observing run (O1), from September 12, 2015 to January 19, 2016, gravitational waves from three binary black hole mergers were detected. The second observing run (O2), which ran from November 30, 2016 to August 25, 2017, saw the first detection of gravitational waves from a binary neutron star inspiral, in addition to the observation of gravitational waves from a total of seven binary black hole mergers, four of which we report here for the first time: GW170729, GW170809, GW170818, and GW170823. For all significant gravitational-wave events, we provide estimates of the source properties. The detected binary black holes have total masses between 18.6þ3.2 −0.7 M⊙ and 84.4þ15.8 −1360 Mpc. No neutron star–black hole mergers were detected. In addition to highly significant gravitational-wave events, we also provide a list of marginal event candidates with an estimated false-alarm rateterm less than 1 per 30 days. From these results over the first two observing runs, which include approximately one gravitational-wave detection per 15 days of data searched, we infer merger rates at the 90% confidence intervals of 110 − 3840 Gpc−3 y−1 for binary neutron stars and 9.7 − 101 Gpc−3 y−1 for binary black holes assuming fixed population distributions and determine a neutron star–black hole merger rate 90% upper limit of 610 Gpc−3 y−1.

−11.1 M⊙ and range in distance between 320þ120

−110 and 2840þ1400

DOI: 10.1103/PhysRevX.9.031040

Subject Areas: Astrophysics, Gravitation

I. INTRODUCTION The first observing run (O1) of Advanced LIGO, which took place from September 12, 2015 until January 19, 2016, saw the first detections of gravitational waves (GWsterm) from stellar-mass binary black holes (BBHs) [1–4]. After an upgrade and commissioning period, the second observing run (O2) of the Advanced LIGO detectors [5] commenced on November 30, 2016 and ended on August 25, 2017. On August 1, 2017, the Advanced Virgo detector [6] joined the observing run, enabling the first three-detector observations of GWsterm. This network of ground-based interferometric detectors is sensitive to GWsterm from the inspiral, merger, and ringdown of compact binary coalescencesterm·dom (CBCs), covering a frequency range from about 15 Hz up to a few kilohertz (see Fig. 1). In this catalog, we report 11 confident detections of GWsterm from compact binary mergers as well as a selection of less significant triggersterm from both observing runs. The observations reported here and future GW detections will shed light on binary

*Full author list given at the end of the article.

Published by the American Physical Society under the terms of license. the Creative Commons Attribution 4.0 International Further distribution of this work must maintain attribution to the author(s) and the published article’s title, journal citation, and DOI.

formation channels, enable precision tests of general relativity (GR) in its strong-field regime, and open up new avenues of astronomy research.

The events presented here are obtained from a total of three searches: two matched-filter searchesterm·mth, PyCBCterm [7,8] and GstLALterm [9,10], using relativistic models of GWsterm from CBCs, as well as one unmodeled searchterm·mth for short-duration transient signals or bursts, coherent WaveBurst (cWBterm) [11]. The two matched-filter searchesterm·mth target GWsterm from compact binaries with a redshifted total mass Mð1 þ zÞ of 2–500 M⊙ for PyCBCterm and 2–400 M⊙ for GstLALterm, where z is the cosmological redshift of the source binary [12], and with maximal dimensionless spins of 0.998 for black holes (BHs) and 0.05 for neutron stars (NSs). The results of a matched-filter search for sub-solar-mass compact objects in O1 can be found in Ref. [13]; the results for O2 will be discussed elsewhere. The burst search cWBterm does not use waveform models to compare against the data but instead identifies regions of excess power in the time-frequency representation of the gravitational strainpersName. We report results from a cWBterm analysis that is optimized for the detection of compact binaries with a total mass less than 100 M⊙. A different tuning of the cWBterm analysis is used for a search for intermediate-mass BBHs with total masses greater than 100 M⊙; the results of that analysis are discussed elsewhere. The three searches reported here use different methodologies to identify GWsterm from compact binaries

All searches have undergone improvements since O1, making it scientifically valuable to reanalyze the O1 data in order to reevaluate the significance of previously identified GW events and to potentially discover new ones. The searches identified a total of ten BBH mergersterm·dom and one binary neutron star (BNS) signal. The GW events GW150914, GW151012 [14], GW151226, GW170104, GW170608, GW170814, and GW170817 have been reported previously [4,15–18]. In this catalog, we announce four previously unpublished BBH mergersterm·dom observed during O2: GW170729, GW170809, GW170818, and GW170823. We estimate the total mass of GW170729 to be 84.4þ15.8 the highest-mass BBH observed to date. GW170818 is the second BBH observed in triple coincidence between the two LIGO observatories and Virgo after GW170814 [16]. As the sky location is primarily determined by the differences in the times of arrival of the GW signal at the different detector sites, LIGO-Virgo coincident events have a vastly improved sky localizationterm, which is crucial for electromagnetic follow-up campaigns [19–22]. The reanalysis of the O1 data did not result in the discovery of any new GW events, but GW151012 is now detected with increased significance. In addition, we list 14 GW candidate events that have an estimated false-alarm rateterm (FAR) less than 1 per 30 days in either of the two matched-filter analyses but whose astrophysical origin cannot be established nor excluded unambiguously (Sec. VII).

−11.1 M⊙, making it

Gravitational waves from compact binaries carry information about the properties of the source such as the masses and spins. These can be extracted via Bayesian inference by using theoretical models of the GW signal that describe the inspiral, merger, and ringdown of the final object for BBH

[23–30] and the inspiral (and merger) for BNS [31–33]. Such models are built by combining post-Newtonian calculations [34–38], the effective-one-body formalism [39–44], and numerical relativity [45–50]. Based on a variety of theoretical models, we provide keypersName source properties of all confident GW detections. For previously reported detections, we provide updated parameter estimates which exploit refined instrumental calibration, noise subtraction (for O2 data) [51,52], and updated amplitude power spectral density estimates [53,54].

The observation of these GW events allows us to place constraints on the rates of stellar-mass BBH and BNS mergers in the Universe and probe their mass and spin distributions, putting them into astrophysical context. The nonobservation of GWsterm from a neutron star–black hole binary (NSBHterm·dom) yields a stronger 90% upper limit on the rate. The details of the astrophysical implications of our observations are discussed in Ref. [55].

This paper is organized as follows: In Sec. II, we provide an overview of the operating detectors during O2, as well as the data used in the searches and parameter estimation. Section III briefly summarizes the three different searches, before we define the event selection criteria and present the results in Sec. IV. Tables I and II summarize some keypersName search parameters for the clear GW detections and the marginal eventsterm. Details about the source properties of the GW events are given in Sec. V, and the values of some important parameters obtained from Bayesian inference are listed in Table III. We do not provide parameter for marginal eventsterm. An independent consistency analysis between the waveform-based results and the data is performed in Sec. VI. In Sec. VII, we describe how the probability of astrophysical origin is calculated and give its value for each significant and marginal event in Table IV. We provide an updated estimate of binary merger rates in this

estimation results

A variety of additional information on each event, data products including strainpersName data and posterior samples, and postprocessing tools can be obtained from the accompanying data release [56] hosted by the Gravitational Wave Open Science Center [57].

II. INSTRUMENTAL OVERVIEW AND DATA

A. LIGO instruments

The Advanced LIGO detectors [58,59] began scientific operations in September, 2015 and almost immediately

detected the first gravitational waves from the BBH merger GW150914.

Between O1 and O2, improvements were made to both LIGO instruments. At LIGO-Livingston (LLO), a malfunctioning temperature sensor [60] was replaced immediately after O1, contributing to an increase in the BNS range from approximately 60 Mpc to approximately 80 Mpc [61]. Other major changes included adding passive tuned mass dampers on the end test mass suspensions to reduce ringing up of mechanical modes, installing a new output Faraday isolator, adding a new in-vacuum array of photodiodes for stabilizing the laser intensity, installing

031040-3

B. P. ABBOTTpersName et al.

PHYS. REV. X 9, 031040 (2019)

higher quantum-efficiency photodiodes at the output port, and replacing the compensation plate on the input test mass suspension for the Y arm. An attempt to upgrade the LLO laser to provide higher input power was not successful. During O2, improvements to the detector sensitivity continued, and sources of scattered lightterm noise were mitigated. As a result, the sensitivity of the LLO instrument rosepersName from a BNS range of 80 Mpc at the beginning of O2 to greater than 100 Mpc by the run’s end.

The LIGO-Hanford (LHO) detector had a range of approximately 80 Mpc as O1 ended, and it was decided to concentrate on increasing the input laser power and forgo any incursions into the vacuum system. Increasing the input laser power to 50 W was successful, but since this increase did not result in an improvement in sensitivity, the LHO detector operated with 30 W input power during O2. It was eventually discovered that there was a point absorber on one of the input test mass optics, which we speculate led to increased coupling of input “jitter” noise from the laser table into the interferometer. By use of appropriate witness it was possible to perform an offline noise sensors, subtraction on the data, leading to an increase in the BNS range at LHO by an average of about 20% over all of O2 [51,52].

On July 6, 2017, LHO was severely affected by a 5.8 the magnitude earthquake in Montana. Postearthquake, sensitivity of the detector dropped by approximately 10 Mpc and remained in this condition until the end of the run on August 25, 2017.

B. Virgo instrument

Advanced Virgo [6] aims to increase the sensitivity of the Virgo interferometer by one order of magnitude, and several upgrades were performed after the decommissioning of the first-generation detector in 2011. The main modifications include a new optical design, heavier mirrors, and suspended optical benches, including photodiodes in a vacuum. Special care was also taken to improve the decoupling of the instrument from environmental disturbances. One of the main limiting noise sources below 100 Hz is the thermal Brownian excitation of the wires used for suspending the mirrors. A first test performed on the Virgo configuration showed that silica fibers would reduce this contribution. A vacuum contamination issue, which has since been corrected, led to failures of these silica suspension fibers, so metal wires were used to avoid delaying Virgo’s participation in O2. Unlike the LIGO instruments, Virgo has not yet implemented signal recycling, which will be installed in a later upgrade of the instrument.

After several months of commissioning, Virgo joined O2 on August 1, 2017 with a BNS range of approximately 25 Mpc. The performance experienced a temporary degradation on August 11 and 12, when the microseismic activity on site was highly elevated and it was difficult to keep the interferometer in its low-noise operating mode.

C. Data

Figure 1 shows the BNS ranges of the LIGO and Virgo instruments over the course of O2 and the representative amplitude spectral density plots of the total strainpersName noise for each detector.

We subtract several independent contributions to the instrumental noise from the data at both LIGO detectors [51]. For all of O2, the average increase in the BNS range from this noise subtraction process at LHO is approximately 20% [51]. At LLO, the noise-subtraction process targeted narrow line features, resulting in a negligible increase in the BNS range.

Calibrated strainpersName data from each interferometer are produced online for use in low-latency searches. Following the run, a final frequency-dependent calibration is generated for each interferometer.

For the LIGO instruments, this final calibration benefits from the use of postrun measurements and the removal of instrumental lines. The calibration uncertainties are 3.8% in amplitude and 2.1° in phase for LLO and 2.6% in amplitude and 2.4° in phase for LHO. The results cited in this paper use the full frequency-dependent calibration uncertainties described in Refs. [64,65]. The LIGO timing uncertainty of <1 μs [66] is included in the phase correction factor.

The calibration of strainpersName data produced online by Virgo has large uncertainties due to the short time available for measurements. The data are reprocessed to reduce the errors by taking into account better calibration models obtained from postrun measurements and the subtraction of frequency noise. The reprocessing includes a time dependence for the noise subtraction and for the determination of the finesse of the cavities. The final uncertainties are 5.1% in amplitude and 2.3° in phase [67]. The Virgo calibration has an additional uncertainty of 20 μs originating from the time stamping of the data.

During O2,

the individual LIGO detectors had duty factors of approximately 60% with a LIGO network duty factor of about 45%. Times with significant instrumental disturbances are flagged and removed, resulting in about 118 days of data suitable for coincident analysis [68]. Of these data, about 15 days are collected in coincident operation with Virgo, which after joining O2 operated with a duty factor of about 80%. Times with excess instrumental noise, which is not expected to render the data unusable, are also flagged [68]. Individual searches may then decide to include or not include such times in their final results.

III. SEARCHES

The search results presented in the next section are obtained by two different, largely independent matchedfilter searches, PyCBCterm and GstLALterm, and the burst search cWBterm. Because of the sensitivity imbalance between the Advanced Virgo detector as compared to the two Advanced LIGO detectors, neither PyCBCterm nor cWBterm elect to analyze

031040-4

GWTC-1: A GRAVITATIONAL-WAVE TRANSIENT CATALOG …

PHYS. REV. X 9, 031040 (2019)

data from Virgo. GstLALterm, however, includes Virgo into its search during the month of August. The two matched-filter searchesterm·mth assume sources that can be modeled by general relativity and, in particular, quasicircular binaries whose spin angular momenta are either aligned or antialigned with their orbital angular momenta. They are still capable, however, of detecting many systems that exhibit precession [69]. In contrast, the cWBterm search relies on no specific physical models of the source waveform, though in results presented here it does impose a restriction that signals are “chirping” in the time-frequency plane. We therefore refer to it as weakly modeled. In the remainder of this section, we present a brief description of each of these searches, summarizing both the parameter space searched and improvements made since their use in O1 [4].

A. The PyCBCterm search

A pipeline to search for GWsterm from CBCs is constructed using the PyCBCterm software package [7,8]. This analysis performs direct matched filtering of the data against a bank of templateterm waveforms to calculate the signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) for each combination of detector, templateterm waveform, and coalescence time [70]. Whenever the local maximum of this SNR time series is larger than a threshold of 5.5, the pipeline produces a single-detector trigger associated with the detector, the parameters of the templateterm, and the coalescence time. In order to suppress triggersterm caused by high-amplitude noise transients (“glitchesterm”), two signal-based vetoesterm·mth may be calculated [71,72]. Using the SNR, the results of these two vetoes, and a fitting and smoothing procedure designed to ensure that the rate of single-detector triggersterm is approximately constant across the search parameter space, a single-detector rank ϱ is calculated for each single-detector trigger [73].

After generating triggersterm in the Hanford and Livingston detectors as described above, PyCBCterm finds two-detector coincidences by requiring a trigger from each detector associated with the same templateterm and with coalescence times within 15 ms of each other. This time window time between the maximum light-travel accounts for LHO and LLO as well as the uncertainty in the inferred coalescence time at each detector. Coincident triggersterm are assigned a ranking statistic that approximates the relative likelihood of obtaining the event’s measured trigger parameters in the presence of a GW signal versus in the presence of noise alone [73]. The detailed construction of this network statistic, as well as the single-detector rank ϱ, is improved from the corresponding statistics used in O1, partially motivating the reanalysis of O1 by this pipeline. Finally, the statistical significances of coincident triggersterm are quantified by their inverse false-alarm rateterm (IFAR). This rate is estimated by applying the same coincidence procedure after repeatedly time shifting the triggersterm from one detector and using the resulting coincidences as a backtrigger is ground sample. Each foreground coincident

assigned a false-alarm rateterm (FAR) given by the number of background triggersterm with an equal or larger ranking, divided by the total time searched for time-shifted coincidences. For an event with a given IFAR observed in the data of duration T, the probability of obtaining one or more equally highly ranked events due to noise is

p ¼ 1 − e−T=IFAR:

ð1Þ

In the analysis of this paper, the data are divided into analysis periods that allow at least 5.2 days of coincident data between the two LIGO detectors [74]. Though previous publications performed time shifting across larger amounts of time [1,2,4], the results here consider only time shifts within a given analysis period, which is done because the noise characteristics of the detector vary significantly from the beginning of O1 through the end of O2, so this restriction more accurately reflects the variation in detector performance. This restriction means, however, the minimum bound on the false-alarm rateterm of candidates that have a higher ranking statistic than any trigger in the background sample is larger than it would be if longer periods of data are used for the time-shift analysis.

that

For the PyCBCterm analysis presented here, the templateterm bank described in Ref. [75] is used. This bank covers binary systems with a total mass between 2 and 500 M⊙ and mass ratios down to 1=98. Components of the binary with a mass below 2 M⊙ are assumed to be neutron stars and have a maximum spin magnitude of 0.05; otherwise, the maximum magnitude is 0.998. The high-mass boundary of the search space is determined by the requirement that the waveform duration be at least 0.15 s, which reduces the number of false-alarm triggersterm from short instrumental glitchesterm. The waveform models used are a reduced-order-model (ROM) [29,76–78] of SEOBNRv4 [29] for systems with a total mass greater than 4 M⊙ and TaylorF2 [38,80] otherwise.

B. The GstLALterm search

A largely independent matched-filter pipeline based on the GstLALterm library [9,10] (henceforth GstLALterm) also performs a matched-filter search for CBC signals. GstLALterm produces triggersterm for each templateterm waveform and each detector by maximizing the matched-filter SNR ρ over onesecond windows and requiring that it exceed a threshold of 4 for the two LIGO detectors and 3.5 for Virgo. The relatively lowerpersName Virgo SNR threshold is an ad hoc choice designed to improve the network sensitivity of the search given Virgo’s smaller horizon distance. For the search described here, candidates are formed by requiring a temporal coincidence between triggersterm from the same templateterm but from different detectors, with the coincidence window set by the light-travel time between detectors plus 5 ms [81]. GstLALterm ranks candidates using the logarithm of the likelihood ratio, L, a measure of how likely it is to observe that candidate if a signal is present compared to if

031040-5

B. P. ABBOTTpersName et al.

PHYS. REV. X 9, 031040 (2019)

[9,82,83]. The noise model

only noise is present is constructed, in part, from single-detector triggersterm that are not found in coincidence, so as to minimize the possibility of contamination by real signals. In the search presented here, the likelihood ratio is a function of ρ, a signalconsistency test, the differences in time and phase between the coincident triggersterm, the detectors that contribute triggersterm to the candidate, the sensitivity of the detectors to signals at the time of the candidate, and the rate of triggersterm in each of the detectors at the time of the candidate [9]. This function the is an expansion of the parameters used to model likelihood ratio in earlier versions of GstLALterm and improves the sensitivity of the pipeline used for this search over that used in O1.

The GstLALterm search uses Monte Carlo methods and the likelihood ratio’s noise model to determine the probability of observing a candidate with a log likelihood ratio greater to log L, Pðlog L(cid:4) ≥ log LjnoiseÞ. The than or equal expected number of candidates from noise with log least as high as log L is then likelihood ratios at NPðlog L(cid:4) ≥ log LjnoiseÞ, where N is the number of observed candidates. The FAR is then the total number of expected candidates from noise divided by the live time of the experiment, T, and the p value is obtained by assuming the noise is a Poisson process:

FAR ¼

p ¼ 1 − e−NPðlog L(cid:4)≥log LjnoiseÞ:

ð3Þ

For the analysis in this paper, GstLALterm analyzes the same periods of data as PyCBCterm. However, FARs are assigned using the distribution of likelihood ratios in noise computed from marginalizing Pðlog L(cid:4) ≥ log Ljnoise; periodÞ over all analysis periods; thus, all of O1 and O2 are used to inform the noise model for FAR assignment. The only exception is GW170608. The analysis period used to estimate the significance of GW170608 is unique from the other ones [17], and thus its FAR is assigned using only its local background statistics.

For this search, GstLALterm uses a bank of templates with a total mass between 2 and 400 M⊙ and a mass ratio between 1=98 and 1. Components with a mass less than 2 M⊙ have a maximum spin magnitude of 0.05 (as for PyCBCterm); otherwise, the spin magnitude is less than 0.999. The TaylorF2 waveform approximant is used to generate templates for systems with a chirp mass [see Eq. (5)] less than 1.73, and the reduced-order model of the SEOBNRv4 approximant is used elsewhere. More details on the bank construction can be found in Ref. [84].

C. Coherent WaveBurst

Coherent WaveBurst (cWBterm) is an analysis algorithm used in searches for weakly modeled (or unmodeled)

transient signals with networks of GW detectors. Designed to operate without a specific waveform modelterm, cWBterm identifies coincident excess power in the multiresolution time-frequency representations of the detector strainpersName data [85], for signal frequencies up to 1 kHz and durations up to a few seconds. The search identifies events that are coherent in multiple detectors and reconstructs the source sky location and signal waveforms by using the constrained maximum likelihood method [11]. The cWBterm detection statistic is based on the coherent energy Ec obtained by cross-correlating the signal waveforms reconstructed in the two detectors. It is proportional to the coherent network SNR and used to rank each cWBterm candidate event. For an estimation of its statistical significance, each candidate event is ranked against a sample of background triggersterm obtained by repeating the analysis on time-shifted dataterm·mth, similar to the background estimation in the PyCBCterm search. To exclude astrophysical events from the background sample, the time shifts are selected to be much larger than the expected signal delay between the detectors. Each cWBterm event is assigned a FAR given by the rate of background triggersterm with a larger coherent network SNR.

To increase robustness against nonstationary detector noise, cWBterm uses signal-independent vetoes, which reduce the high rate of the initial excess power triggersterm. The primary veto cut is on the network correlation coefficient cc ¼ Ec=ðEc þ EnÞ, where En is the residual noise energy estimated after the reconstructed signal is subtracted from the data. Typically, for a GW signal cc ≈ 1, and for instrumental glitchesterm cc ≪ 1. Therefore, candidate events with cc<0.7 are rejected as potential glitchesterm.

Finally, to improve the detection efficiency for a specific class of stellar-mass BBH sources and further reduce the number of false alarms, cWBterm selects a subset of detected events for which the frequency is increasing with time, i.e., events with a chirping time-frequency pattern. Such a timefrequency pattern captures the phenomenological behavior of most CBC sources. This flexibility allows cWBterm to potentially identify CBC sources with features such as higher-order modes, high mass ratios, misaligned spins, and eccentric orbits; it complements the existing templated algorithms by searching for new and possibly unexpected CBC populations. For events that passed the signal-independent vetoes and chirp cut, the detection significance is characterized by a FAR computed as described above; otherwise, cWBterm provides only the reconstructed waveforms (see Sec. VI).

IV. SEARCH RESULTS

A. Selection criteria

In this section, we motivate and describe the selection of gravitational-wave events for presentation in this paper. We include any candidate event that can be identified with a nontrivial probability of association to an astrophysical binary merger event, as opposed to instrumental noise [86].

031040-6

GWTC-1: A GRAVITATIONAL-WAVE TRANSIENT CATALOG …

PHYS. REV. X 9, 031040 (2019)

The matched-filter and cWBterm search pipelines produce large numbers of candidate events, but the majority of these are of very low significance and have a correspondingly low probability of being of astrophysical origin.

We desire to identify all events that are confidently astrophysical in origin and additionally provide a manageable set of marginal triggersterm that may include some true signals but certainly also includes noise triggersterm. To do this identification, we establish an initial threshold on estimated FAR of 1 per 30 days (about 12.2 per year), excluding any event that does not have a FAR less than this threshold in at least one of the two matched-filter analyses (see Sec. III). The cWBterm search results are not used in the event selection process. At this FAR threshold, if each pipeline produces independent noise events, we would expect on average two such noise events (false alarms) per month of analyzed coincident time. During these first two observing runs, we also empirically observe approximately two likely signal events per month of analyzed time. Thus, for O1 and O2, any sample of events all of whose measured FARs are greater than 1 per 30 days is expected to consist of at least 50% noise triggersterm. Individual triggersterm within such a sample are then considered to be of little astrophysical interest. Since the number of triggersterm with a FAR less than 1 per 30 days is manageable, restricting our attention to triggersterm with lowerpersName FAR captures all confident detections while also probing noise triggersterm.

Within the sample of triggersterm with a FAR less than the ceiling of 1 per 30 days in at least one of the matched-filter searchesterm·mth, we assign the “GW” designation to any event for which the probability of astrophysical origin from either matched-filter search is greater than 50% (for the exact

definition and calculation of the astrophysical probability, see Sec. VII). We list these events in Table I.

For the remaining events in the sample that pass the initial FAR threshold, neither matched-filter search finds a greater than 50% probability of astrophysical origin. These are considered marginal eventsterm and are listed in Table II. The astrophysical probabilities of all events, confident and marginal, are given in Table IV.

B. Gravitational-wave events

Results from the two matched-filter searchesterm·mth are shown in Fig. 2 and that of the unmodeled burst search in Fig. 3. In each plot, we show the observed distribution of events as a function of the inverse false-alarm rateterm, as well as the expected background for the analysis time, with Poisson uncertainty bands. The foreground distributions clearly stand out from the background, even though we show only rightward-pointing arrows for any event with a measured or bounded IFAR greater than 3000 y.

We present more quantitative details below on the 11 gravitational events, as selected by the criteria in Sec. IVA, in Table I. Of these 11 events, seven have been previously reported: the three gravitational-wave events from O1 [1–4] and, from O2, the binary neutron star merger GW170817 [18] and the binary black hole events GW170104 [15], GW170608 [17], and GW170814 [16]. The updated results we report here supersede those previously published. Four new gravitational-wave events are reported here for the first time: GW170729, GW170809, GW170818, and GW170823. All four are binary black hole events.

As noted in Sec. III, data from O1 are reanalyzed because of improvements in the search pipelines and the

For the remainder of this section, we briefly discuss each of the gravitational-wave events, highlighting interesting features from the perspectives of the three searches. A discussion of the properties of these sources may be found in Sec. V. Though the results presented are from the final, offline analysis of each search, for the four new GW events, we also indicate whether the event is found in a low-latency search and an alert sent to electromagnetic observing partners. Where this process did occur, we mention in this paper only the low-latency versions of the three searches with offline results presented here; in some cases, additional low-latency pipelines also found events. A more thorough discussion of all of the low-latency analyses and the electromagnetic follow-up of O2 events may be found in Ref. [22].

1. GW150914, GW151012, and GW151226 During O1, two confident detections of binary black holes were made: GW150914 [1] and GW151226 [2]. Additionally, a third trigger was noted in the O1 catalog of binary black holes [3,4] and labeled LVT151012. That label is a consequence of the higher FAR of that trigger, though detector characterization studies show no instrumental or environmental artifact, and the results of parameter estimation are consistent with an astrophysical BBH source. Even with the significance that is measured with the O1 search pipelines [4], this event meets the criteria of Sec. IVA for a gravitational-wave event, and we henceforth relabel this event as GW151012.

The improved O2 pipelines substantially reduce the FAR assigned to GW151012: It is now 0.17 y−1 in the PyCBCterm search (previously, 0.37 y−1) and 7.92 × 10−3 y−1 in the GstLALterm search (previously, 0.17 y−1). These improved FAR measurements for GW151012 are the most salient result of the reanalysis of O1 with the O2 pipelines; no new gravitational-wave events were discovered. The first binary black hole observation, GW150914, remains the highest SNR event in O1 and the second highest in the combined O1 and O2 datasets, behind only the binary neutron star inspiral GW170817.

Recently, Ref. [88] appeared. That catalog also presents search results from the PyCBCterm pipeline for O1 and also finds GW150914, GW151012, and GW151226 as the only confident gravitational-wave events in O1, with identical bounds on FAR to the PyCBCterm results in Table I for GW150914 and GW151226. In the reanalysis of O2 data, GW170814 is identified as a double-coincident event between LLO and LHO by GstLALterm. This results from the noise subtraction in the LIGO data and updated calibration of the Virgo data. Because of the noise subtraction in the LIGO data, under GstLALterm’s ranking of multiple triggersterm [9], a new templateterm generates the highest ranked trigger as double coincident, with a Hanford SNR of 9.1 (the previous highest ranked trigger, a triple, had 7.3). Though this highest ranked the pipeline does event identify other highly significant triggersterm, some double coincident and some triple coincident. As the search uses a discrete templateterm bank, peaks from the SNR time series of the individual detectors, and clustering of several coincident triggersterm over the bank, it is difficult in this case to tell from the search results alone whether the event is truly a triple-coincident detection. For a definitive answer, we perform a fully Bayesian analysis with and without the Virgo data, similar to the results in Ref. [16]. Comparing the evidence, this Bayesian analysis—which enforces coherence and therefore more fully exploits consistency among detected amplitudes, phases, and times of arrival than the search pipelines—finds that a triple-coincident detection is strongly favored over a double-coincident detection, by a factor of approximately 60. Thus, the updated results are consistent with those that were previously published.

3. GW170817 Across the entirety of O1 and O2, the binary neutron star inspiral GW170817 remains the event with the highest network SNR and is accordingly assigned the most stringent possible bound on its FAR by PyCBCterm and the highest value of L (the logarithm of the likelihood ratio) of any event in the combined O1 and O2 dataset by GstLALterm. As explained in detail in the original detection paper [18], a loud glitch occurs near the end of this signal in LLO. For the matched-filter searchesterm·mth, this glitch is excised via timedomain gating (and that gating is applied consistently to all such glitchesterm throughout O2). Because the cWBterm pipeline is designed to detect short signals, it does not use that gating technique, and it rejects this event because of the glitch.

4. GW170729

We turn now to gravitational-wave events not previously announced. The first of these is GW170729, observed at 18∶56∶29.3 UTC on July 29, 2017. The PyCBCterm pipeline assigns it a FAR of 1.36 y−1, the GstLALterm pipeline a FAR of 0.18 y−1, and the cWBterm pipeline a FAR of 0.02 y−1. As it is identified with the highest significance among all three search pipelines by the weakly modeled pipeline, it is worth investigating whether this event is unusual in some way, exhibiting effects (for instance, precession or higher-order modes) not adequately modeled by the templates used in the matched-filter searchesterm·mth. As a relatively simple way of investigating this question, a comparison study is done between the PyCBCterm pipeline and cWBterm, using software injectionsterm·mth with parameters drawn from the SEOBNRv4 ROM parameter estimation of this event. That waveform does not incorporate precession or higher-order modes, but, by using these samples as inputs to both searches, we can probe how often we see comparable results. It is found that approximately 4% of these SEOBNRv4 ROM samples are recovered by both the PyCBCterm and cWBterm pipelines with FAR ≥ 1 y−1 and FAR ≤ 0.02 y−1, respectively. Thus, the observed difference in FARs between the two pipelines is not exceptionally unlikely and is consistent with a noise fluctuation which happens to decrease the significance of the event as seen by PyCBCterm and increase it for cWBterm. The detailed CBC parameter estimation studies in Sec. V also indicate no significant evidence for observationally important precession or higher-order modes. This event was identified only in the offline analyses, so no alert was sent to electromagnetic partners.

5. GW170809

GW170809 was observed on August 9, 2017 at 08∶28∶21.8 UTC with a FAR of 1.45 × 10−4 y−1 by PyCBCterm and <1.00 × 10−7 y−1 by GstLALterm. This event was identified in low latency by both the GstLALterm and cWBterm pipelines, and an alert was sent to electromagnetic observing partners. In the final offline cWBterm analysis with updated calibration and noise subtracted from LIGO data, this event did not pass one of the signal-independent vetoes (Sec. III C) and was therefore not assigned a FAR.

6. GW170818 GW170818 was observed at 02∶25∶09.1 UTC on August 18, 2017, by GstLALterm with a FAR of 4.20 × 10−5 y−1; it was not observed by either the PyCBCterm or cWBterm pipelines. It is observed as a triple-coincident event by GstLALterm, with an SNR in Virgo of 4.2, a Hanford SNR of 4.1, and a Livingston SNR of 9.7. In the PyCBCterm search, a trigger is seen in the Livingston detector with a comparable SNR and is noted as a “chirplike” single-detector trigger. When the Hanford and Virgo data are analyzed with modified settings around the time of that event, there are triggersterm with a similar SNR to

031040-9

PHYS. REV. X 9, 031040 (2019)

those of GstLALterm and, therefore, well below the threshold of 5.5 needed for a single-detector trigger in the PyCBCterm search to be considered further for possible coincidence. This event is initially identified in low latency by the GstLALterm pipeline as a LLO-Virgo double-detector trigger. Online, the Virgo trigger is not included in significance estimation, and the LLO-only trigger does not pass the false-alarm threshold for the online search.
